   From Wikipedia, the free encyclopedia

   Earth-goddess in Proto-Indo-European mythology

   *Dʰéǵʰōm ([84]Proto-Indo-European: *dʰéǵʰōm or *dʰǵʰōm; lit.
   'earth'),^[85][1]^[86][2] or *Pl̥th₂éwih₂ (PIE: *pl̥th₂éwih₂, lit. the
   'Broad One'),^[87][3]^[88][4] is the reconstructed name of the
   [89]Earth-goddess in the [90]Proto-Indo-European mythology.

   The Mother Earth (*Dʰéǵʰōm Méh₂tēr) is generally portrayed as the vast
   (*pl̥th₂éwih₂) and dark (*dʰengwo-) abode of mortals, the one who bears
   all things and creatures. She is often paired with [91]Dyēus, the
   daylight sky and seat of the never-dying and heavenly gods, in a
   relationship of contrast and union, since the fructifying rains of
   Dyēus might bring nourishment and prosperity to local communities
   through formulaic invocations. Dʰéǵʰōm is thus commonly associated in
   Indo-European traditions with fertility, growth, and death, and is
   conceived as the origin and final dwelling of human beings.

Name and etymology

   [[92]edit]
   Look up [93]Reconstruction:Proto-Indo-European/dʰéǵʰōm in Wiktionary,
   the free dictionary.

   The [94]Proto-Indo-European (PIE) word for 'earth', *dʰ(é)ǵʰōm
   ([95]acc. dʰǵʰ-ém-m, [96]gen. *dʰǵʰ-mós), is among the most widely
   attested words in Indo-European languages (cf. [97]Albanian dhé and
   toka; [98]Hittite tēkan, tagān; [99]Sanskrit kṣám; [100]Greek khthṓn;
   [101]Latin humus; [102]Avestan zam; [103]Tocharian tkaṃ; [104]Old Irish
   dú, [105]Lithuanian žẽmė; [106]Old Slavonic zemlja), which makes it one
   of the most securely reconstructed PIE terms.^[107][1] On the other
   hand, the linguistic evidence for the ritualization of the name
   *dʰéǵʰōm is not systematically spread across the inherited traditions,
   as she also appears under other names and epithets, principally
   *pl̥th₂éwih₂ (the 'Broad One').^[108][3]

   If the PIE Earth-goddess is reliably reconstructed under the name
   *Dʰéǵʰōm, with *pl̥th₂éwih₂ being one of her epithets, she was most
   likely the Earth herself conceived as a divine entity, rather than a
   goddess of the earth,^[109][5] Proto-Indo-European mythology still
   relying on a strong [110]animistic substrate.^[111][6]

Epithets

   [[112]edit]

   Based on comparative analysis of textual and epigraphic evidence,
   [113]historical linguists and [114]philologists have been able to
   reconstruct with a comfortable level of certainty several epithets and
   expressions that were associated with *Dʰéǵʰōm in Proto-Indo-European
   times: *Pl̥th₂éwih₂ (the 'Broad One'), *Dʰéǵʰōm Méh₂tēr
   ('Mother-Earth'), and, in this form or a similar one, *Dʰéǵʰōm Dʰengwo-
   ('Dark Earth').^[115][4]^[116][7]

The Broad One

   [[117]edit]

   The commonest epithet applied to the earth in Indo-European poetic
   traditions is *pl̥th₂éwih₂ (the 'Broad One'), which is the feminine
   form of *pléth₂-us, meaning 'flat, vast, broad'. A group of
   [118]cognates appear in various divine names, including the Vedic
   earth-goddess [119]Pṛth(i)vī, the Greek nymph Plataia, and the Gaulish
   goddess [120]Litavī.^[121][4]^[122][8] The epithet is also attested in
   nearly identical poetic expressions associating *dʰéǵʰōm and
   *pl̥th₂éwih₂: [123]Avestan ząm pərəθβīm ('broad earth'), [124]Sanskrit
   kṣā́m ... pṛthivī́m ('broad earth'), and [125]Old Hittite palḫiš ...
   dagan(-zipaš) ('broad ... earth[-genius]').^[126][7]^[127][9]^[128][10]

   Another similar epithet is the 'All-Bearing One', the one who bears all
   things and creatures.^[129][11] She was also referred to as
   'much-nourishing' or 'rich-pastured' in Vedic, Greek, and Old Norse
   ritual expressions sharing the root *plh₁u- ('much').^[130][3]

   In the Proto-Indo-European cosmology, the earth Dʰéǵʰōm was likely
   perceived as a vast, flat and circular continent surrounded by waters
   ('The Ocean').^[131][8]

Mother Earth

   [[132]edit]

   The Earth-goddess was widely celebrated with the title of 'mother'
   (méh₂tēr), and often paired with *[133]Dyḗus ph[2]tḗr, the
   'sky-father'. She is called annas Dagan-zipas ('Mother Earth-genius')
   in [134]Hittite liturgy, and paired with the Storm-god of heaven, as
   well as Mat' Syra Zemlya ('Mother Moist Earth') in the [135]Russian
   epic poems.^[136][a] To the Vedic goddess of the earth [137]Prithvi is
   often attached the epithet Mata ('mother') in the [138]Rigveda,
   especially when she is mentioned together with [139]Dyaus, the
   sky-father.^[140][13]

   úpa sarpa mātáram Bhū́mim etā́m uruvyácasam Pṛthivī́ṃ suśevām
   Slip in to this Mother Earth, the wide-extending Broad One, the
   friendly...

   — 10.18.10, in The [141]Rigveda, translated by M. L. West.^[142][14]

   The Baltic earth-goddess [143]Zemyna is likewise associated with the
   epithets 'Mother of the Fields' and 'Mother of the Forests'.^[144][15]
   She is also treated respectfully as mother of humans.^[145][b]
   Similarly, the cult of the "Earth Mother" in old Slavic religion and
   traditions associated the earth with the progenetrix's
   role.^[146][c]^[147][d]^[148][19] In a legend from [149]Smolensk, it is
   told that a human has three mothers: a birth mother (rodna) and two
   great (velikih) mothers, Mother Moist Earth and the Mother of
   God.^[150][20] Additionally, the Anglo-Saxon goddess Erce (possibly
   meaning 'bright, pure') is called the 'mother of Earth' (eorþan modor)
   and likely identified with Mother Earth herself in a [151]ritual to be
   performed on an unfruitful plough-land.^[152][13] She is also called
   Fīra Mōdor ('Mother of men') in Old English poetry.^[153][5]

   A similar epithet, Mother of All (Μητηρ Παντων), is ascribed to the
   Greek earth-goddess Gaia, as recorded for instance in [154]Aeschylus'
   Prometheus Unbound (παμμῆτόρ τε γῆ; "Oh! universal mother
   Earth"),^[155][21] and in The Libation Bearers (ἰὼ γαῖα μαῖα; Mother
   Gaia).^[156][22] Likewise, several of the [157]Orphic Hymns attach the
   epithet 'mother' to Earth (γαῖα θεὰ μήτηρ).^[158][23] In a
   [159]Samaveda hymn dedicated to the [160]Vedic fire god [161]Agni, he
   is described as "rapidly ... [moving] along his mother
   earth".^[162][24] In an [163]Atharva Veda hymn (12.1) (Pṛthvī Sūkta, or
   Bhūmī Sūkta), the celebrant invokes [164]Prithvi as his Mother, because
   he is "a son of Earth".^[165][25] The word bhūmi is also used as an
   epithet of Prithvi meaning 'soil' and in reference to a threefold
   division of the universe into heavens, sky, and
   earth.^[166][26]^[167][27]^[168][28] On her own, [169]Bhūmi is another
   Vedic deity with Mother-Earth attributes.^[170][29]^[171][30]^[172][31]

   The Greek goddess of the harvest and agriculture [173]Demeter could
   also be a [174]cognate, possibly deriving from an [175]Illyrian root
   dā- (from *dʰǵʰ(e)m-) attached to māter ('mother'), although this
   proposition remains controversial in scholarship.^[176][13] The Roman
   evidence for the idea of Earth as a mother is doubtful, as it is
   usually associated with the name [177]Terra rather than Tellus (the
   pre-Imperial earth-goddess), and the attested tradition may have been
   influenced by Greek motifs.^[178][32]

   In [179]Albanian the Earth Mother Goddess or Great Mother ([180]Magna
   Mater) is simply referred to as [181]Dhé "Earth", and traces of her
   worship have been preserved in [182]Albanian tradition.^[183][33]

Dark Earth

   [[184]edit]

   Another [185]Proto-Indo-European epithet, *dʰéǵʰōm dʰengwo- or *dʰéǵʰōm
   dʰṇgu- ('dark earth'), can be reconstructed from the Hittite formulae
   dankuiš dagan-zipaš ('dark genius of the earth') and dankuš tēkan,
   which were frequently used to name the underworld, but sometimes also
   the earth's surface, and partially from the [186]Albanian and
   [187]Slavonic expressions dhe të zi ('black earth') and *črnā(yā) zemyā
   ('dark earth'), which have retained the term
   *dʰéǵʰōm.^[188][34]^[189][11]^[190][7] Other reflexes can be found in
   Greek Gaia Melaina (γαîα μέλαινα; 'black earth'), or in [191]Old Irish
   domun donn ('brown earth').^[192][11]^[193][35] A Lithuanian expression
   takes the form "may the black earth not support me".^[194][36]

   ištamašta=an=ma palḫiš dankuiš daganzipaš
   ...the broad dark earth heard him...

   — 4.4 Rs. 12ff, in [195]Keilschrifturkunden aus Boghazköi [[196]de],
   translated by J. L. García Ramón.^[197][7]

   In Latvian dainas about plant fertility, the color black symbolized a
   good and abundant harvest, and the black soil was considered the most
   fertile.^[198][37] In a [199]Russian fairy tale, the maiden is buried
   "under a blanket of black earth".^[200][38]

   A formula *čṛnā(jā) zemjā ('dark earth') can be reconstructed based on
   expressions found in the southern [201]Slavic-speaking area, in ritual
   and burial contexts, like Ukrainian čorna zemlicja (in a Christmas
   carol); Slovene černa zemlja (in incantations); Bulgarian černata (in
   relation to the Earth, in curses), oženich se zadevojka černozemka
   (metaphor for 'to die young'), "черна земя" ('black earth');^[202][39]
   Serbo-Croatian zagrlila (poljubila) ga je (crna) zemlja (meaning 'he
   has died'); Serbian crna zemlja.^[203][40]

   In another line of scholarship, the formula of the dark earth seems to
   be related to invocation or oaths, where the announcer summons the
   Earth as an observer or witness, as seen by [204]Solon's elegiac
   Fragment 36.^[205][41] The Slavic deity 'Moist Earth' (Syra Zemlya) was
   similarly invoked during oaths and called to witness in land
   disputes.^[206][42]

Role

   [[207]edit]

Mating with the Sky Father

   [[208]edit]

   The Earth goddess was conceived as the dark dwelling of mortals, in
   contrast with [209]Dyēus, the bright diurnal sky and the seat of the
   gods.^[210][11]^[211][e] Both deities often appear as a pair, the Sky
   Father (*Dyḗws Ph₂tḗr) uniting with Mother Earth (*Dʰéǵʰōm Méh₂tēr) to
   bring fertility and growth.^[212][14]^[213][44] The Earth is thus often
   portrayed as the giver of good things: she is exhorted to become
   pregnant in an [214]Old English prayer, and Slavic peasants described
   Zemlja as a prophetess that shall offer favourable harvest to the
   community.^[215][5]^[216][45] The unions of Zeus with Semele and
   Demeter is similarly associated with fertility and growth in [217]Greek
   mythology.^[218][45] According to Jackson, however, Dʰéǵʰōm is "a more
   fitting partner of [219]Perk^wunos than of Dyēus", since the former is
   commonly associated with fructifying rains as a [220]weather
   god.^[221][3]

   The Earth–Heaven couple was probably not at the origin of the other
   heavenly gods. The [222]Divine Twins and [223]H[2]éwsōs seem to have
   been conceived by [224]Dyēus alone,^[225][46] since they are mentioned
   through the formulaic expressions *Diwós Népoth[1]e ('Descendants of
   Dyēus') and *Diwós D^huǵh[2]tḗr ('Daughter of Dyēus),
   respectively.^[226][47]

Anatolian

   [[227]edit]

   In [228]Hittite mythology, the Storm God of Heaven, one of the most
   important in the Hittite pantheon,^[229][48] has been syncretized with
   local [230]Anatolian or [231]Hattian deities, merging with a local
   storm god with terrestrial characteristics. At a later point, the Storm
   God of Heaven was paired with local goddess Wurulemu, with chthonic
   traits.^[232][49]

Indo-Aryan

   [[233]edit]

   In the Vedic texts, [234]Prithvi the mother is usually paired with
   [235]Dyaus the father,^[236][50]^[237][14] as shown for instance in
   [238]Samaveda hymns.^[239][51]^[240][52] Due to their complementary
   relationship, they are celebrated as universal parents.^[241][53]
   However, other texts of sacred literature attribute different partners
   to the Earth goddess: in an Atharveda Hymn (12.1), Prithvi is coupled
   with [242]Parjanya ([243]Sanskrit: पर्जन्य, parjánya), a deity of rain
   and fertilizer of earth.^[244][54]^[245][55] In the same hymn, verse 6
   (12.1.6), [246]Indra, another Vedic deity of thunder and rain, is
   described as "consort" and protector of Earth.^[247][56]

   According to [248]Herodotus, the [249]Scythians considered Earth to be
   the wife of Zeus.^[250][45]

Graeco-Roman

   [[251]edit]

   Zeus is associated with [252]Semele, a possible descendant of Dʰéǵʰōm,
   but also with Demeter, which could be another cognate stemming from the
   Mother Earth.^[253][45]^[254][5] In the Danaids, [255]Aeschylus
   describes how Ouranos and Chthôn are seized by a mutual desire for
   sexual intercourse: the rain falls, then Earth conceives and brings
   forth pasture, cereal crops, and foliage.^[256][45] Likewise, "Heaven
   and Earth" regularly appear as a duo among deities invoked as witnesses
   to Hittite treaties, and the Roman [257]Tellus Mater is paired with
   [258]Jupiter in [259]Macrobius's [260]Saturnalia.^[261][14]

   The mating of Zeus and female characters with chthonic elements
   (Démeter) or associated with earth (such as Semele, Plataia and Themis)
   may be a remnant of the Sky/Earth coupling.^[262][45] Other religious
   expressions and formulas in [263]Greek cultic practice attest to a
   wedding or union between a sky-god and an earth-mother: the Homeric
   Hymn to Gaia calls her "Wife of Starry Ouranos";^[264][57] weddings in
   [265]Athens were dedicated to both Ouranos and Gaia;^[266][58] an
   [267]Orphic Hymn tells that the cultist is both "a child of Earth and
   starry Sky";^[268][59] in Athens, there was a statue of Gaia on the
   Acropolis depicting her beseeching Zeus for rain;^[269][60]^[270][f]
   Zeus Chthonios and Gê Chthonia form a cultic pair in
   [271]Mykonos;^[272][62]^[273][63] Zeus is invoked with an Earth Mother
   partner by their priestesses in [274]Dodona;^[275][64]^[276][65] a
   funerary epigram of one Lycophron of Pherai, son of Philiskos, states
   he shall live "among the stars uplifted by his father" (Zeus), while
   his body "occupies mother earth".^[277][66]

   In the cosmogony of [278]Pherecydes of Syros, male deity Zas
   (identified with Zeus and the celestial/heavenly heights) unites with
   female character Chthonie (associated with the earth and the
   subterranean depths) in sacred rites of marriage, a union that appears
   to hark back to "the theology of the rites of
   fertility-fecundity"^[279][67] and lays the foundation of the
   cosmos;^[280][68]

   Ancient Roman scholar [281]Varro, in his book De re rustica, listed
   five divine pairs, among which Juppiter, "father", and Tellus, "the
   Earth mother", both responsible for the fruitfulness of
   agriculture.^[282][69]

Norse

   [[283]edit]

   In [284]Norse mythology, the goddess [285]Jörd, a [286]jötunn
   (giantess) whose name means 'earth' (from Proto-Germanic *erþō-,
   'earth, [287]soil, land'), begets the thunder-god [288]Thor (Donar)
   with [289]Odinn–not a sky-god, although a chief god of the Norse
   pantheon.^[290][70] A line in the [291]Gylfaginning by Norse poet
   [292]Snorri Sturluson mentions that the Earth is both daughter and wife
   ("Jörðin var dóttir hans ok kona hans") of the All-Father,^[293][71]
   identified as Odinn.

Slavic

   [[294]edit]

   Russian scholar O. G. Radchenko points that remnants of the coupling
   exist in East Slavic riddles, incantations and herb charms.^[295][43]
   As pointed by scholarship, Croatian historian [296]Natko Nodilo saw an
   occurrence of the Masculine Heavens and Feminine Earth in the riddle
   Visok tata, plosna mama, bunovit zet, manita devojka ("Tall father, fat
   mother, rebellious son-in-law, frenzied maiden"), about the components
   of the world, and whose answer is "Sky, Earth, Wind and Fog".^[297][72]
   In a Russian incantation (Beschwörungsformel), heaven and earth are
   referred to as a father/mother pair: Ty nebo otec; ty zemlja mat'.
   ("You Heaven are father; you Earth are mother").^[298][73]^[299][g] A
   folk expression "plaskófka matka, vysoki tatka" refers to "the low,
   flat earth" in contrast with "the highest sky".^[300][75]

   Polish scholarship also indicates some holdover of the idea exists in
   the folklore of [301]Poland, for instance, in folk riddle Matka nisko,
   ojciec wysoko, córka ślepa, syn szalony ("A mother low down, a father
   high up, a blind daughter and a mad son"), whose answer is "earth,
   heaven, night, wind".^[302][76]^[303][h]

   In a charm collected in Arkhangelsky and published in 1878 by historian
   Alexandra Efimenko ([304]ru), the announcer invokes "Mother-Earth"
   (Земля мать) and "Father Heaven" (небо отец).^[305][78] According to
   researcher Natalya Polyakova, there was among the Slavs an old belief
   that earth was fertilized by the heavenly rains and that it was a sin
   to profane her. If this happened, the heavenly father would no longer
   send her rains, and thus would cause drought.^[306][79]

Baltic

   [[307]edit]

   Baltic scholarship recognizes in ancient Baltic beliefs a division of
   the world into a heavenly half, with masculine and dynamic attributes
   and associated with light and celestial bodies, and an earthly half,
   feminine and static, related to plants and waters.^[308][80]

   According to Lithuanian ethnologue [309]Nijolė Laurinkienė [[310]lt],
   in Baltic tradition, it was said that the earth closed off (as in
   "sleeping" or "hibernating") near the end of autumn/beginning of
   winter, and "opened up" with the coming of the spring - a season when
   the first rains begin to fall. For this reason, it was believed that
   Baltic thunder god [311]Perkūnas acted as the "opener" of the earth
   with his rains, making the grass grow and bringing life anew. In later
   tradition, it seems this deity was replaced by [312]Saint George
   (Jurgis, Yurja, Sveti Juraj), who, in folksongs, was described as
   opening the earth in the spring with a key.^[313][81]^[314][i]

Final dwelling of mortals

   [[315]edit]

   Dʰéǵʰōm had a connection with both death and life: the deceased are
   made from her and shall eventually return to her, but the crop also
   grows from her moist soil fertilized by the rain of Dyēus.^[316][84]
   This points to a hierarchical conception of the status of mankind
   regarding the heavenly gods, confirmed by the widespread use of the
   term 'mortal' as a synonym of 'human' rather than 'living species' in
   Indo-European traditions.^[317][85] In a Hittite military oath, the
   earth is said to drink the blood of the fallen ("This not wine, it is
   your blood, and as the earth has swallowed this..."), as in Aeschylus'
   [318]Seven Against Thebes (736) and in the Indian [319]Mahabharata
   ("... the earth shall drink today the blood of their king").^[320][86]

   The word for 'earth' underlies the many formations for designating
   humans, because they are seen as 'earthly' or fashioned from the earth
   itself.^[321][87] It is reconstructed in the derivative forms
   *dʰǵʰ(e)-mōn and *dʰǵʰom-yos, which underwent a [322]semantic shift
   from 'earthling' to 'human': Sanskrit jmán ('from the earth') and
   kṣámyaḥ ('earthly'), Latin [323]homō ('man'), Gothic guma ('man'), Old
   Lithuanian žmuõ ('man') and Old Prussian smoy ('man'), [324]Old Irish
   duine ('man'), and [325]Gaulish -xtonio (*gdonios 'man'?).^[326][88]
   The [327]Neo-Phrygian term zemelōs (ζεμελως) is interpreted as meaning
   'men', or 'terrestrial' as opposed to 'heavenly'.^[328][89]^[329][90]
   In the words of linguist [330]Antoine Meillet, those [331]metaphors go
   back to a time when it was "natural to designate 'humans' by the
   distinctive features that distinguish them from the gods: mortality,
   life on earth".^[332][91]

Albanian

   [[333]edit]
   [334][220px-Gjama.jpg] Practicing of [335]Gjâma, the Albanian
   traditional lamentation of the dead, by the men of [336]Theth
   ([337]Shala) in the funeral of Ujk Vuksani, 1937.

   In Albanian tradition the Earth – [338]Dheu or Toka – is deeply
   respected so that she would carefully receive the dead in her
   chest.^[339][92] For instance, during the last phase of the Albanian
   traditional mourning practice – [340]Gjâma – after a usual lament, the
   mourners sit on their knees in a row and continuing the last call of
   the dead person, they sit on the ground, put their foreheads upon the
   earth and caress the earth with their hands, as if they want to express
   love and care for the earth. They stay like this until someone of the
   house, specifically charged with this task, goes and lifts them
   up.^[341][93]

   In all Albanian lands the burial custom required to put a metal coin in
   the grave, inserting it in the dead's hand or mouth, or on one side of
   the body. A general explanation was that it served "to pay for the
   place of the grave" or "to pay the Earth so that she keeps the dead
   inside her". This is a reflection of the cult of the Earth, associated
   "with the place of the new dwelling in the [342]eternal life", with the
   coin representing a symbolic gift to the Earth. Coins of this type have
   also been found by archaeologists in the graves of the Albanians in the
   Middle Ages and in those of the [343]Illyrians in antiquity.^[344][92]

Greek

   [[345]edit]

   In a religious context, Chthôn (Χθών) was conceived as the nether land
   of the underworld deities and the dead (Iliad 6,411; 8,14; Theogony
   119; etc.), and often as the world itself as opposed to the
   sky.^[346][89]

   Another reflex of Dʰéǵʰōm as the mother of mortals and their final
   resting place may also be found in Demetrioi ('of Demeter'), an
   Athenian designation for the dead,^[347][94] and in [348]Aeschylus's
   verses in Choephori 127: "Yea, summon Earth, who brings all things to
   life, / And rears and takes again into her womb."^[349][64]^[350][95]
   In addition, Demeter was worshipped in some Greek cities in relation to
   her connection to the Underworld (cf. epithet [351]Chthonia, 'of the
   earth, underworld'), besides her typical association with grains and
   crops.^[352][96]^[353][97] Demeter was also associated with the role of
   ward^[354][98] or mother of the dead:^[355][99] according to
   [356]Plutarch's On the Face in the Moon's Orb, Demeter, who rules over
   the earth and all earthly things, separates the soul from the body
   after a human dies.^[357][100]

   A similar imagery is described by poet [358]Euripides, in his play
   [359]The Suppliants, lines 530-536: "Let the dead now be buried in the
   earth, / and each element return to the place from where it came to the
   body, / (...) the body to the ground; / for in no way did we get it /
   for our own, but to live our life in, and after that its mother earth
   must take it back again".^[360][101]^[361][102] A funerary epigram of
   one Lycophron of Pherai, son of Philiskos, states his body, given by
   mother, now "occupies mother earth" (μητέρα γἥν).^[362][103]

Baltic

   [[363]edit]

   Moreover, historical sources on [364]Baltic mythology, specially on
   Lithuanian and Latvian religions and practices, describe the dual role
   of goddesses Zemyna and Zemes Mate: while they were connected to the
   fertility of the land, they were also associated with receiving the
   dead and acting as their ruler and guardian.^[365][104]^[366][j]

   In Latvian dainas, Zemes Mate is associated with fellow [367]Mahte
   ("Mothers") Velu Mate ('Mother of Dead Souls') and Kari Mate ('Mother
   of Graves'). According to researcher Elza Kokare, Zemes Mate and Kari
   Mate act as the resting places of the dead, guarding its body and
   holding the key to their graves.^[368][106] As an individual character,
   Zemes mate is invoked as a person's final resting
   place.^[369][k]^[370][l]^[371][m] Pieces of Lithuanian folklore also
   make references to Earth as mother of humans and their final abode
   after death.^[372][112]^[373][n]^[374][o]^[375][p]

   Funeral lamentations, such as some collected in Veliouna in the 19th
   and 20th centuries, attest the expression "sierą žemelę" as the
   destination of the deceased to whom the lament is
   dedicated.^[376][116]^[377][117]^[378][118]^[379][119] In a later
   military death lament, the "sierą žemelę" is said to drink the blood of
   the fallen soldier, after being shot.^[380][120] An issue of Lithuanian
   newspaper [381]Draugas published a dainas wherein the person invokes
   the earth as "žeme, žeme, siera žemele", and asks it to take her, a
   maiden, having already taken father and mother ("Atėmei tėvą ir
   motinėlę"), but the earth scolds her.^[382][121]

Slavic

   [[383]edit]

   Old Slavic beliefs seem to attest some awareness of this ambivalent
   nature of the Earth: it was considered men's cradle and nurturer during
   one's lifetime, and, when the time of death came, it would open up to
   receive their bones, as if it were a "return to the
   womb".^[384][122]^[385][q]^[386][r]^[387][s]^[388][t]^[389][u]^[390][v]

   In Polish curses, the malediction is aimed towards "the Holy Earth"
   (święta ziemia) not receiving the remains of the person cursed (as in,
   Bodaj cię święta ziemia nie przyjęła! and Oby cię święta ziemia nie
   przyjęła!).^[391][129] Researcher Anna Engelking cited that scholar
   Boris Uspensky wrote "a comprehensive analysis of the mythical trope of
   holy earth: the mother of humankind, which gives birth to people and
   accepts their bodies after death".^[392][130] Similarly, the imagery
   appears in "funeral hymns and speeches", e.g., Powracasz w ziemię, co
   twą matką była,/ Teraz cię strawi, niedawno żywiła ("You return to
   earth that has been your mother,/ She has fed you so far, now you’ll be
   devoured").^[393][131]

   The imagery of the terre humide ("moist earth") also appears in funeral
   lamentations either as a geographical feature (as in Lithuanian and
   Ukrainian lamentations)^[394][132] or invoked as Mère-Terre humide
   ("Mother Moist
   Earth").^[395][133]^[396][134]^[397][w]^[398][x]^[399][y]^[400][z]^[401
   ][aa] The imagery and expression of "Mother Moist Earth" seem to have
   persisted well into the 21st century, although divorced from its sacral
   aspect.^[402][ab]

   In a Ukrainian lamentation, the mourner invokes earth as his "damp
   mother" ("Земле ж моя земле, мать сирая"), and asks it to take him, the
   mourner ("a young one"), since it has already taken father ("отця") and
   mother ("неньку", endearing or diminutive form of "не́ня").^[403][144]

   In [404]Belarusian folk songs, the earth is invoked as "syroj
   ziamli-matušcy" ('damp earth-mother'), and even referred to as the
   resting place of the mourner's loved one ("Žoŭcieńki piasok, syraja
   ziamlia, Tut pachavana milaja maja"; [405]English: 'Yellow sand, damp
   earth: here my dear is buried').^[406][145] In addition, phraseological
   studies by linguist Olga A. Lyashchynskaya ([407]be) recognize the
   incidence of the expression in [408]Belarusian: expression "спаць у
   сырой зямлі" ('to sleep in the damp earth') is a metaphor for
   death;^[409][146] expression "ляжаць у сырой зямлі/зямельцы" ("to lie
   in the damp earth/ground") denotes a burial ("to be
   interred");^[410][147] "ажаніцца з <сырою> зямлёй" ("to marry the
   [moist] earth") means "to die".^[411][148]

   [412]Serbian idiomatic expressions also associate the earth with the
   grave, and the formula "dark earth" ("crna zemlja") appears in
   reference to the resting place of the dead.^[413][149]^[414][150]

   Mat' Syra Zemlya is also invoked in wedding songs by the orphan bride
   for her parents to bless her journey to the new household.^[415][151]

Indo-Aryan

   [[416]edit]

   In Book 10 of the Rigveda, Hymn XVIII (a funeral hymn), verses 10-13,
   the earth is invoked to receive the body of the departed and to cover
   him gently, as a mother does a child: "10. Betake thee to the lap of
   Earth the Mother, of Earth far-spreading, very kind and gracious. (...)
   11. Heave thyself, Earth, nor press thee downward heavily: afford him
   easy access, gently tending him. Cover him, as a mother wraps her skirt
   about her child, O Earth."^[417][152]^[418][153]^[419][154] A second
   hymn in Vedic sacred literature requests Earth to open up and
   explicitly receive the dead, while also mentioning the "two kings",
   Yama and Varuna: "Open thy arms, o Earth, receive the dead/ With gentle
   pressure and with loving welcome / Embrace him tenderly, e'en as a
   mother / Folds her soft vestment round the child she loves. / Soul of
   the dead, depart (...)".^[420][155]^[421][53]

   In the [422]Śatapatha Brāhmaṇa, written by Vedic sage [423]Yajnavalkya,
   there is reference to a ritual of the placement of the bones of the
   deceased in the earth after cremation.^[424][156] According to the
   Kanda XIII,8,3,3, the text says that "May Savitri deposit thy bones in
   the mother's lap [māturupastha].' Savitri thus deposits his bones in
   the lap of the mother [māturupastha], this earth [pṛthivyai]; 'O Earth,
   be thou propitious unto him!'".^[425][157]

Evidence

   [[426]edit]

*Dʰéǵʰōm

   [[427]edit]

   Cognates stemming from *dʰéǵʰōm are attested in the following
   mythologies:
     * [428]PIE: *dʰ(é)ǵʰ-ōm ([429]acc. dʰǵʰ-ém-m, [430]gen. *dʰǵʰ-mós),
       the 'earth',^[431][1]^[432][2]^[433][158]
          + [434]Anatolian: *déǵ-m,^[435][158]
               o [436]Hittite: Tagān-zepa- ('Genius of the Earth; later
                 dagān-zipa-), a chthonic deity also serving as a witness
                 in treaties (composed of the stem dagan-, 'earth',
                 attached to šipa,
                 'genius'),^[437][42]^[438][34]^[439][159]
          + [440]Proto-Greek: *kʰtʰōn, 'earth' (the initial [441]consonant
            cluster *dʰǵʰ- evolved into *kʰtʰ- via [442]metathesis; the
            final -m turned into -n through regular [443]sound
            laws),^[444][160]
               o [445]Greek: Chthonie, attested in fragmentary passages of
                 [446]Pherecydes of Syros as a primordial goddess of earth
                 who changed her name to [447]Gaia after Zeus married
                 her;^[448][161] she is depicted as Chthôn (Χθών), the
                 partner of [449]Ouranos in [450]Aeschylus' Danaids; the
                 same name is also used as an epithet of Poseidon by
                 [451]Homer;^[452][89] the epithets [453]Chthonía (Χθωνία;
                 'belonging to the earth, the ground, the underworld'),
                 associated with the grain goddess
                 [454]Demeter,^[455][162]^[456][163] and Chtónios
                 (Χθόνιος; id.), attached to [457]Zeus or [458]Hermes as
                 those who go to the underworld;^[459][160]^[460][164]
                 another cognate appears in the name of the [461]chthonic
                 deities (χθόνιοι θεοί) of the underworld,^[462][165]
          + [463]Old Avestan: [464]Zām ('Earth'), a sanctified being in
            the [465]Zoroastrian tradition that embodies the concept of
            Earth,^[466][166]^[467][167]^[468][ac]
               o Young Avestan: Zamyād (a contraction of *zām huδād
                 [469]yazad), divinity of the Munificent Earth in the
                 Zamyād [470]Yašt.^[471][170]^[472][171]^[473][172]
          + [474]Balto-Slavic: *źem- (from*dʰǵʰ-em-),^[475][173]
               o [476]Baltic: *žeme,^[477][174]
                    # [478]Lithuanian: [479]Žemyna (also Žemynėlė and
                      Žemelė),^[480][175] a goddess celebrated as the
                      bringer of flowers, and a recipient of prayers and
                      sacrifices;^[481][42]^[482][5]^[483][176] expression
                      sierà žěmė ('wet/humid earth');^[484][177]^[485][ad]
                    # [486]Latvian: Zemes Māte ('Mother Earth'), one of
                      the goddesses of death in Latvian
                      mythology,^[487][5]^[488][180]
               o [489]Slavic: *zeml'à,^[490][173]^[491][ae]
                    # [492]East Slavic:
                         @ [493]Old Russian: [494]Mat' Syra Zemlya
                           ('Mother Moist Earth'), in the [495]byliny
                           (epic poems), and Matushka zeml'ja ('Little
                           Mother Earth')^[496][182] in folk incantations
                           to ensure a good harvest;^[497][183]
                         @ [498]Ukrainian: Syraja zemlja and mati - syra
                           zemlja;^[499][184]^[500][af]
                         @ [501]Belarusian: Maci syra zjamlja ("Маці сыра
                           зямля"),^[502][185]^[503][184] and expression
                           "The Sacred Earth, it is our mother" ("Зямля
                           святая, яна наша маці");^[504][184]^[505][187]
                    # [506]West Slavic:
                         @ [507]Polish: Mateczka Ziemia ("Little mother
                           earth");^[508][ag] matka ziemia, ziemicka
                           mamicka and do ziemi matusi.^[509][184]
                    # [510]South Slavic:
                         @ [511]Bulgarian: Sura Zemya ('Moist Earth'), in
                           folk songs,^[512][42]^[513][189]^[514][ah] and
                           surova zemya ("Сурова
                           земя");^[515][191]^[516][192]^[517][193]
                         @ [518]Serbian: mother Earth ("маjка-земља"), in
                           an [519]idiomatic
                           expression;^[520][194]^[521][195]^[522][196]^[5
                           23][ai]
          + [524]Proto-Albanian: *dzō,^[525][198]
               o [526]Albanian: dhe ('earth'), appearing in [527]Dhé,
                 Zonja e Dheut "Earth Great Mother", "Earth Goddess"
                 respectively,^[528][199] and ritualized in the cult of
                 the earth and [529]oath swearings (beja me
                 dhe),^[530][200]^[531][201] also appears in euphemisms
                 like Dhetokësi, Dheu or Përdhesi used to refer to the
                 serpent as an earth-deity,^[532][202] and [533]E Bukura e
                 Dheut ('Beauty of the Earth'), a chthonic
                 goddess.^[534][203]^[535][204]^[536][205]

   Two parallel terms meaning 'human, earthling' are also attested as
   derivatives of the stem *dʰ(é)ǵʰ-:
     * PIE: *dʰǵʰom-yo-, 'human, earthling',^[537][206]
          + [538]Sanskrit: kṣámyaḥ, 'terrestrial',^[539][89]
          + Proto-Greek: *kʰtʰōnyos,
               o [540]Greek: chtónios (χθόνιος), 'belonging to the
                 earth',^[541][160]^[542][89]
          + [543]Celtic: *gdonyo-, 'human, person',^[544][206]
               o [545]Gaulish: -xtonio- (*gdonios), 'human', in the
                 expression deuogdonioi,
                 'gods-and-men',^[546][207]^[547][206]
               o [548]Old Irish: duine, 'human',^[549][206]
               o [550]Middle Welsh: dyn, 'human',^[551][206]
     * PIE: dʰǵʰ(e)-mōn (acc. *dʰǵʰ-m-on-m), 'human,
       earthling',^[552][208]^[553][209]^[554][210]
          + [555]Italic: *χem-ō-, *χe/om-on-m,^[556][208]
               o [557]Latin: homō, hominis, 'human, man',^[558][208]
               o [559]Oscan: humuns, [560]Umbrian: homonus,
                 'man',^[561][208]
          + [562]Germanic: *guman-, 'man',^[563][209]
               o [564]Gothic: guma, 'man',^[565][209]
               o [566]Old Norse: gumi, 'man',^[567][209]
               o [568]Old English: guma, [569]Old Saxon: gumo, [570]Old
                 High German: gomo, 'man',^[571][209]
          + [572]Baltic:
               o [573]Old Lithuanian: žmuō, 'man'; Lith.: žmónės,
                 'people', žmonà, 'wife, woman',^[574][210]
               o [575]Old Prussian: smoy, 'man', smūni,
                 'person',^[576][210]

   Additionally, remnants of the noun *dʰéǵʰōm can be found in formulaic
   phrases and religious epithets:^[577][aj]
     * [578]Vedic: the compound Dyāvākṣamā, ('heaven and earth'), with
       kṣamā associated with the earth goddess [579]Prithvi (the 'Broad
       One').^[580][42]
     * [581]Greek: the epithets χαμύνη (khamyne ([582]de), 'of the land'),
       in reference to Deméter^[583][ak] (in [584]Pausanias
       6.21.1),^[585][213]^[586][214]^[587][215] and Χαμοναῖα (khamynaia,
       'on the ground').^[588][216]^[589][217] A designation Χαμοναῖος
       (khamonaïos, 'of the ground'; 'of the earth') in reference to Zeus,
       is also attested.^[590][218]^[591][219] These epithets are
       considered cognates to χαμαί (khamaí, 'pertaining, belonging to the
       earth').^[592][213]^[593][220]^[594][221]

Possible reflexes

   [[595]edit]

   Other mythologies may show the presence of characters and expressions
   that are etymologically cognate to *Dheghom. However, these cognates
   are less secured:
   [596][206px-Sebastiano_Ricci_-_Dionysus_%281695%29.jpg] [597]Jove and
   [598]Semele (1695) by [599]Sebastiano Ricci.
     * [600]Anatolian:
          + [601]Lydian: references to a cult of [602]Men Tiamou ('of
            Tiamos') led scholars to believe Tiamou is an epithet that
            means 'of the Earth' or 'of the Netherworld', possibly
            connected to [603]Luwian tiyamm(i) 'earth'.^[604][222] This
            expression would be equivalent to a common epithet of Men:
            καταχθονιος ('of the Underworld';
            'subterranean').^[605][223]^[606][224]^[607][225]^[608][226]
     * [609]Hellenic:
          + [610]Doric: linguist Krzysztof Witczak suggests the dialectal
            Doric word "δηγῆ" dēgê, in the expression "δηγῆ και σιωπᾷ"
            ("earth and keeps silence"), is a possibly ancient loanword
            from [611]Proto-Albanian.^[612][227]
          + [613]Greek: Damia, one of the [614]Horae, a minor deity
            related to spring, growth and vegetation, and usually paired
            with fellow Horae Auxesia.^[615][228]^[616][229] Ancient
            literature suggests it might have been another name for
            Demeter.^[617][230]^[618][231]
     * [619]Iranian:
          + [620]Khotanese: evidence suggests that the Khotanese preserved
            some relics of an Indo-Iranian worship of the earth, as seen
            in the Saka roots ysam- and ysama-, both meaning 'earth' and
            cognate to Avestan zam-.^[621][232] The word is also attested
            in the personal name Ysamotika,^[622][233] and in the
            religious expression ysamaśśandaā, meaning
            'world'.^[623][234]^[624][235]^[625][236]
     * [626]Tocharian: the expression tkamñkät (Tocharian A) and keṃ-ñäkte
       (Tocharian B) are used in religious Buddhist texts written in the
       Tocharian languages, where it denotes the earth or an 'earth-god'
       of some sort.^[627][237]^[628][238]^[629][239]
     * [630]Italic:
          + Hunte, an [631]Umbrian deity, possibly stemming from *ǵʰom-to-
            'who is below'.^[632][2]
          + [633]Semonia, obscure deity associated with crops and
            sowing,^[634][240] of possible [635]Roman or [636]Sabine
            origin and worship, usually attested with the epithet
            [637]Salus Semonia.^[638][241] A possible male counterpart is
            [639]Semo Sancus,^[640][242] god of Sabine provenance whose
            traits merged with Dius Fidius's. Semonia and Sancus appear
            with other agricultural/crop deities Seia and
            Segetia.^[641][243]
     * [642]Celtic:
          + [643]Old Irish: goddess [644]Dana, taken by some
            Indo-Europeanist scholars to be an Irish earth
            goddess.^[645][244]^[646][245]
          + [647]Welsh mythology: linguist [648]John T. Koch interprets
            the family known as Children of Dôn (Plant Dôn) as "Children
            of the Earth", since the name of their matriach, [649]Dôn,
            would derive from Celtic *gʰdʰonos ('the earth',
            [650]gen.).^[651][246]
     * [652]Baltic:
          + [653]Lithuanian: Žemėpatis^[654][247]^[655][248] ('Earth
            Spouse')^[656][249] and Žemininkas,^[657][250] male deities
            associated with cattle, agriculture and the fertility of the
            land.^[658][251] Their names are present in historical records
            of the Lithuanian non-Christian faith by foreign
            missionaries.^[659][252] A male divinity with the name
            Zemeluks, Zamoluksei,^[660][253] Zameluks or
            Ziameluks^[661][254]^[662][255] is also said to be attested.
            An account tells he is a DEUS TERRAE ('earth god'),^[663][256]
            while in other he is "a lord or god of earth who was buried in
            the earth" by the Prussians.^[664][257]
     * [665]Unclassified Indo-European languages:
          + [666]Phrygian: the epithet ΓΔΑΝ ΜΑ (Gdan Ma), taken to mean
            'Earth Mother',^[667][258] or a loan from [668]Anatolian
            languages.^[669][244]^[670][259] However, the name appears as
            a compound in names of [671]Asia Minor written in the
            [672]Greek alphabet.^[673][260] Phrygian also attests the word
            KTON as referring to the earth.^[674][258]
          + [675]Thracian: Zemelā (possibly from *gʰem-elā); with a
            cognate in the Greek goddess
            [676]Semele,^[677][42]^[678][5]^[679][al]^[680][am] and the
            obscure [681]Dionysian epithet Semeleios^[682][263] (Semeleius
            or Semelēus),^[683][264] meaning 'He of the Earth', 'son of
            Semele'.^[684][265]^[685][266]^[686][267]^[687][268]^[688][an]
          + [689]Messapic: Damatura, from dā- (possibly from *dʰǵʰ(e)m-)
            attached to matura ('mother'); maybe at the origin of the
            Greek goddess [690]Demeter.^[691][270]

*Pl̥th₂éwih₂

   [[692]edit]

   Cognates stemming from the epithet *Pl̥th₂éwih₂ (the 'Broad One') are
   attested in the following traditions:
     * [693]Old Hittite: palḫiš dankuiš daganzipaš, 'broad dark
       earth-genius',^[694][7]
     * [695]Indo-Iranian: *pṛtH-uiH-,^[696][271]
          + Vedic: [697]Pṛithvī Mātā (पृथ्वी) ('Mother Earth, the Vast
            One'), the most frequent Vedic word for both the earth and the
            Earth-goddess;^[698][5]^[699][4] and the poetic formula kṣā́m
            ... pṛthivī́m ('broad earth'),^[700][7]^[701][10] cf. also
            [702]Prithu ([703]Sanskrit: पृथु, Pṛthu), a mythological
            sovereign who chases the goddess Prthvi, shapeshifted as a
            cow; his name means 'far, wide, broad'.^[704][272]^[705][273]
          + [706]Young Avestan: ząm pərəθβīm, 'broad
            earth',^[707][9]^[708][7]^[709][10]
     * [710]Greek: Plátaia (Πλάταια), a naiad described as consort of Zeus
       and the daughter of the river [711]Asopos; also the name given to
       the city of [712]Plataea in [713]Beotia,^[714][4]
     * [715]Celtic: [716]Litavī, probably an earth-goddess; also the
       divine name given to the peninsula of [717]Brittany in medieval
       Celtic languages,^[718][4]^[719][274]

   The word also survived in common terms for 'land, field':
     * [720]Germanic: *fuldō, 'earth, ground, field, the world',^[721][9]
          + [722]Old Norse: fold; [723]Old English: folde, same
            meaning.^[724][10]^[725][9]^[726][ao]
     * [727]Proto-Slavic: *pȍļe, 'field',^[728][275]
          + [729]Slavic mythology: Polevoi or [730]Polevik, male deity of
            the fields;^[731][276] Polianitsa, name of a female
            [732]bogatyr related to *pȍļe;^[733][277]^[734][278] Byely
            Polianin ('White Field-Dweller'), a folktale
            character;^[735][279]
          + [736]Old Church Slavonic: [737]polje, 'field'.^[738][275]

   [739][250px-Statue_of_Goddess_or_Queen_at_Monas.JPG] Statue of [740]Ibu
   Pertiwi, a non-Indo-European descendant of *Pl̥th₂éwih₂

   In non-Indo-European traditions, a notable descendant of *Pl̥th₂éwih₂
   occurs as [741]Ibu Pertiwi; her name borrowed from Vedic Pṛithvī, she
   is a [742]national personification of [743]Indonesia.^[744][280]

   Other cognates are less secured:
     * [745]Baltic:
          + [746]Latvian: expression plata māte ('wide mother'), present
            in Latvian riddles about natural features and whose answer is
            "žeme" ('earth').^[747][281]^[748][282]
     * [749]Italic:
          + [750]Venetic: pletuvei, attested in a funerary
            monument;^[751][283] and Pultovia.^[752][284]^[753][285]
          + [754]Vestinian: [755]Peltuinum.^[756][286]^[757][285]
          + [758]Umbrian: Pletinas, an epithet assigned to Italic goddess
            [759]Cupra.^[760][286]^[761][287]
     * [762]Celtic:
          + [763]Celtiberian: letontu; Letondonis (attested as a personal
            name);^[764][288] leitasama (superlative form of adjective,
            attested on a coin).^[765][289]^[766][290]
          + [767]Gallaecian: Bletisam(a) (modern [768]Ledesma,
            Salamanca),^[769][291] attested in a rock carving inscribed in
            a tombstone.
          + [770]Hispano-Celtic: the toponym [771]Ledaña ([772]es) (in
            [773]Cuenca, Spain), thought to derive from
            *(p)litanya.^[774][292]
          + [775]Gaulish: Litana,^[776][293] toponym describing "a vast
            forest"^[777][294] located in North Italy, which, according to
            historiographer [778]Titus Livius, "was named Litania by the
            Gauls"; the site of the historical [779]Battle of Silva
            Litana.
          + [780]Gallo-Roman: Litanobriga,^[781][295]^[782][296] a Celtic
            settlement of unknown location.

Parallels

   [[783]edit]

   Although not considered a cognate to either Dʰéǵʰōm or Pl̥th₂éwih₂,
   deity [784]Spenta Armaiti, of [785]Zoroastrism religion, is associated
   with the earth,^[786][297]^[787][298] with fertility^[788][299] and
   farmers^[789][ap]^[790][aq] as well as the dead.^[791][302]

   A counterpart exists in pre-Christian [792]Armenian mythology, named
   [793]Spandaramet or [794]Sandaramet, whose role is connected to earth
   and the underworld.^[795][303] Namely, she was the "Armenian
   earth-goddess" of vineyards,^[796][304] but also ruled over "'those
   that are asleep', i.e. the dead".^[797][305] She is considered to have
   been developed from her Zoroastrian counterpart, [798]Spenta Armaiti, a
   female being in that tradition. Spandaremet was transformed into a male
   god of the Underworld in later Armenian tradition,^[799][306] and,
   under Christian influence, lent her name to an underworld realm where
   evil spirits are said to dwell.^[800][304]^[801][307]

   Both deities are seen, in their respective religions, as the wife or
   companion of a sky-god, [802]Ahura
   Mazda^[803][308]^[804][309]^[805][310]^[806][ar] or [807]Aramazd. He,
   in turn, is said to be the deity of rains in some accounts.^[808][312]

See also

   [[809]edit]
     * [810]Earth goddess

Footnotes

   [[811]edit]
    1. [812]^ Professor Małgorzata Oleszkiewicz-Peralba states the
       character of "Mother Moist Earth" was worshipped by the
       [813]Eastern Slavs, while a deity called Holy Earth was venerated
       by the [814]Western Slavs.^[815][12]
    2. [816]^ "According to Lithuanian myths, the god Perkunas is the
       brilliant son of Zemyna, Mother Earth (Zeme in Lithuanian is
       'Earth'). She is the beginning of all: waters come from her depths,
       plants are rooted in her, animals have their existence, their lives
       and their support from her; men, too, call her their mother.
       'Whoever hits earth with a stick, hits his own mother.'."^[817][16]
    3. [818]^ "(...) In Ancient Russia, as V. L. Komarovic has
       convincingly demonstrated, the cult of the Earth, Mother Earth, was
       very narrowly linked to the cult of the ancestors, of the clan
       ('rod'). The earth was the mother of the family and of the clan.
       She was seen as a motherly body that brings forth and nurtures
       children. Sometimes, she is described as a person: stones were her
       body, roots her bones; trees and grass her hair. (...) Both for the
       Slavs and for the [819]Varangians, she was the source of fertility
       and prosperity, and of political authority. (...)"^[820][17]
    4. [821]^ "[Mother Earth] was also the maternal breast and womb; the
       black and fruitful soil. As such she was thought to carry the
       family, the village, and the nation, which she nurtured and
       protected - and ruled - like a mother.
       'Mother Earth,' wrote [822]Fedotov, 'becomes clearer to us against
       the background of the gens religion.' Through her the individual
       and the tribal ancestors were united and made whole; she conferred
       identity upon each person: 'The sacred motherhood of the earth is
       intimately akin to the worship of the parents. (...)' She was also
       the genetrix of the family and the clan."^[823][18]
    5. [824]^ According to Gritsay, Russian scholars E. V. Antonova and O.
       G. Radchenko mention that the sky is associated with a paternal
       figure and the elements of light and fire (e.g, epithet
       "свет-батюшка" 'luminous father'), in contrast with a Mother-type
       character, associated with water (moisture) and earth, and also
       called "темная" and "черная" ('dark', 'black').^[825][43]
    6. [826]^ On that note, the epithet ὀμβροχαρής ('delighting in rain')
       - a [827]Hapax legomenon - is attached to the Earth in an Orphic
       Hymn.^[828][61]
    7. [829]^ "Some remnants of fertilizing rites still point in the
       direction that there might have been a marriage between earth and
       heaven in the primitive mythology of Eastern Slavs".^[830][74]
    8. [831]^ "In folk cosmogonic myths, the male sky fertilises the earth
       (SSSL 1(2): 17–56), the act being described as "the sacred union
       (hierogamy) between the Sky-God and the Earth-Mother" (Eliade 1961,
       after Cummings n.d.). (...) Echoes of the hierogamy can be found in
       a Polish folk riddle: "Father shoots but doesn't kill; mother eats,
       though it has no mouth", which stands for the heaven–rain–earth
       complex. In peasant poems, heaven embraces the earth with love: at
       the crossroads, where Christ dies, heaven embraces the earth and
       presents the Mother of Bread with a herb-and-wheat
       wreath."^[832][77]
    9. [833]^ The worship of Saint George with the coming of springtime
       also occurs in [834]South Slavic tradition.^[835][82]^[836][83]
   10. [837]^ "Zemyna (otherwise Zemlja or Perkunatelé) is the
       earth-goddess and psychopomp of the dead."^[838][105]
   11. [839]^ Best exemplified by mythologist [840]Lotte Motz: "The
       chthonic goddess zemes mate (Mother Earth) receives the dead within
       her realm. In dainas addressed to her, she provides the eternal
       resting place: "Rock me mother, hold me mother! / Short is the time
       spent at your breast. / Mother Earth will hold me longer, / beneath
       her turf, a welcome guest." (J1209)".^[841][107] She also stated
       that "In Latvian society ... Mother Earth - zemes mate - is chiefly
       the resting place of the departed, ..."^[842][108]
   12. [843]^ "In the next quatrain folksong it concerns about death, the
       sleeping (slumbering) in the grave. The Mother Earth is the
       goddess, from whom are coming all living beings and to whom after
       death they go back: (25) Ar Dieviņu, mâmulïte, / Labvakar, zemes
       mate!/ Labvakar, zemes mate, / Vai büs laba dusesanal [Good bye,
       Mother, / Good evening, Mother Earth! / Good evening, Mother Earth,
       / Shall I slumber well?]".^[844][109]
   13. [845]^ "Ar Dieviņu, tēvs, māmiņa,/ Labvakaru, Zemes māte (x2)/
       Glabā manu augumiņu". [Farewell, father and mother, / Good evening,
       Earth mother (x2) / Take my body in your
       keeping].^[846][110]^[847][111]
   14. [848]^ "Archeological findings witness that the most ancient phase
       of Lithuanian culture was definitely Zemyna's culture. The
       distinguishing factor in these findings was the burial rites. In
       the oldest cultural phase, the dead were buried - given back to
       Zemyna, Mother Earth."^[849][113]
   15. [850]^ Researcher Nijole Laurinkiene, at the end of her book on
       Zemyna, writes thus: "Žemyna was also imagined as the giver and
       supporter of human life, because like flora and fauna, humanity is
       a part of nature. (...) The newborn would immediately be laid down
       on Mother Earth as if she were its biological mother, so that she
       could ‘accept’ and ‘embrace’ the infant as her own earthly creation
       and give it vegetative power and vitality on a cosmic plane.
       (...)".^[851][114]
   16. [852]^ "Žeme, motina mano, aš iš tavęs esu, tu mane šeri, tu mane
       nešioji, tu mane po smerčio pakavosi" [Earth, my mother, I am from
       you, you feed me, you support me, you embrace me after
       death].^[853][115]
   17. [854]^ For instance: "the Russian peasant envisioned the underworld
       of the ancestors as a house heated against the dampness of Mother
       Moist Earth by a pech [pečʹ, 'stove'].";^[855][18] "Among the
       peasantry in Vladimir Province, as in other places, it was
       customary for the dying to ask earth permission to reenter her body
       with the ritual invocation: 'Mother Moist Earth, forgive me and
       take me'."^[856][123]
   18. [857]^ "The peasant child who died left its natal mother and went
       back to 'mother earth'. (...) That Russians did (and still do)
       personify the earth as a mother is well known. The peasant topos
       'mother moist earth' ('mat' syra zemlia') refers to the mother
       specifically as a place one goes after dying, or in order to die
       (as opposed to a fertile place which gives birth to a harvest - for
       which there are other topoi). Ransel speaks of peasant beliefs
       about the earth pulling the child back to itself, inviting death.
       (...) To resist death too much is to resist 'mother moist
       earth'."^[858][124]
   19. [859]^ "According to a Polish legend, "God ordered the Earth: ‘You
       will give birth to people and you will devour them; whatever you
       give birth to, you will eat, as it is yours’" (Szyjewski 211,
       130).".^[860][125]
   20. [861]^ "Mother Earth stands at the core of the Eastern Slavic
       religiosity. In her converge the most secret and profound religious
       feelings. With awe, the people venerate the black, moist depths -
       the womb which is the source of all fertilized powers, the
       nourishing breast of nature, the definitive resting place of all in
       death. Mother Earth is covered by a veil of grass, flowers,
       forests, trees, vegetables and grain. Thus both beauty and
       fertility are her choice virtues and powers. As a mother who
       nourishes living human beings, the earth is the embodiment of
       kindness and mercy; she also embraces them for rest after
       death."^[862][126]
   21. [863]^ "Symbolically, funeral rites provide the belief that the
       deceased will return to mother earth to live a new life in a new
       abode (the coffin and grave). According to Russian folk belief, the
       deceased no longer lives in its former home but continues a liminal
       existence in a new "dwelling-place," that is the coffin, which in
       some parts of Russia even had windows (Vostochnoslavianskaia 348).
       (...) In this context, the motif of life in the funeral lament is
       similar to the archetypal figure of the Moist Mother Earth (Mati
       syra zemlia) in its representation of rebirth. In these laments,
       the deceased is portrayed as being returned to the Moist Mother
       Earth, but before settling in her "permanent nest" it is carried
       into its new room—the coffin. С попом—отцом духовныим / Да с петьем
       божьим церковныим! / Как схороним тебя, белая лебедушка, / Во
       матушку сыру землю / И во буеву холодную могилушку, / В вековечну,
       бесконечну тебя жирушку, / Закроем тебя матушкой сырой землей, /
       Замуравим тебя травонькой шелковою! (Chistov 237) [With a priest,
       with a spiritual father / And with the swimming of God's Church /
       How will we bury you, little white swan / In the Damp Mother Earth
       / In the cold little grave / In the eternal, heavenly home / We
       will cover you with the Damp Mother Earth / We will cover you with
       silk grass]. (...) Funeral rituals, thus, reinforced the link
       between the living and the departed while allowing the deceased to
       rest permanently in its new domicile—the cosmic womb that is the
       [864]Moist Mother Earth."^[865][127]
   22. [866]^ "[867]East Slavic [868]paganism was the product of a
       landlocked agricultural empire. Gods of sun, moon, stars, and wind
       did exist, but prayers were directed down to the life-giving black
       soil rather than up to celestial deities. Bodies did not "rise"
       after death but were reabsorbed into the womb of
       [869]Mat'-syra-zemlya, Moist Mother Earth".^[870][128]
   23. [871]^ For example: "The maiden fair is dead (...) Split open, damp
       Mother Earth! / Fly asunder, ye coffin planks!";^[872][135] "A
       young sergeant prayed to God, / Weeping the while, as a river
       flows,/ For the recent death of the Emperor, / The Emperor, Peter
       the First. / And thus amid his sobs he spake, - / 'Split asunder, O
       damp mother Earth / On all four sides - / Open, ye coffin planks
       (...)'";^[873][136] "All on my father's grave / A star has fallen,
       has fallen from heaven ... / Split open, O dart of the thunder, The
       moist mother Earth!";^[874][137] "I will take my dear children [and
       see], / Whether moist Mother Earth will not split open. / If moist
       Mother Earth splits open, / Straightway will I and my children bury
       ourselves in it (...) Split open, moist Mother Earth, / And be thou
       open, O new coffin-planks (...) (a widow's lament)";^[875][138]
       "Arise, O ye wild winds, from all sides! Be ye borne, O winds, into
       the Church of God! Sweep open the moist earth! Strike, O wild
       winds, on the great bell! Will not its sounds and mine awaken words
       of kindness" (an orphan's lament).^[876][138]
   24. [877]^ The expression is also mentioned in a saying from Olonets:
       the master of the house invites his ghostly visitor to warm itself
       by the fire of the pech, since it must have been cold for him
       staying "in the moist earth".^[878][139]
   25. [879]^ In an adjuration by a [880]Raskol, the supplicant invokes
       her to forgive them: "Forgive me, O Lord; forgive me, O holy Mother
       of God; (...) forgive, O damp-mother-earth; (...)".^[881][140]
   26. [882]^ In a funeral lament collected in the Olonets region by
       scholar Barsov, the mourner cries for a man struck by lightning
       sent by "thunderous" Saint Ilya, when said man was supposed to
       perform his Christian duties: "They lit candles of bright wax, /
       They prayed to God diligently, / They bowed low to moist mother
       earth / (...) The sinful soul departed without repentance/ (...)/
       [His body] will not be committed to moist mother earth."^[883][141]
   27. [884]^ "Were you not afraid, had you no fear of entering
       mother-moist-earth? For it is cold there, and there is hunger
       there.".^[885][142]
   28. [886]^ Professors Elizabeth Warner and Svetlana Adonyeva published
       in 2021 a book with the results of a joint research on funeral
       laments in modern Russian rural places. Their findings show that
       the laments still contained the poetical expression. For instance:
       "(...) Well, I know myself, little orphan girl, / Where you are
       going, all dressed up,/ You are on your way into
       mother-moist-earth..."; "(...) Break asunder, mother-moist-earth./
       (...)/Stand up, my darling child. / Hear me, your grieving mother,
       (...)".^[887][143]
   29. [888]^ Sometimes Zam is paired with other Zoroastrian deity,
       [889]Armaiti, another being associated with the Earth,^[890][168]
       thus forming a [891]compound Zam-Armaiti or Zam-Armatay.^[892][169]
   30. [893]^ The word sierà means the color 'gray' in Lithuanian, and
       scholarship suggests it is phonetically - not semantically - close
       to Russian syra 'wet, moist'. However, it is also acknowledged that
       the expression "gray earth" may still indicate the fertility of the
       land by referring to a combination of the elements of earth and
       water (humidity). Works that contain this expression have been
       collected from eastern and southern Lithuania.^[894][178] On the
       other hand, historian Rainer Eckert claimed the word sierà 'damp'
       is a borrowing from [895]East Slavic syra.^[896][179]
   31. [897]^ A common epithet that accompanies 'earth' in Slavic
       languages is syra 'moist, damp'. Claire Le Feuvre suggests that the
       word is etymologically related to [898]Old Icelandic saurr and
       [899]Greek language 'eúroeis', used to describe the Underworld and
       the burial place of mortals, and all three words derive from a
       [900]Proto-Indo-European poetical expression that means "damp
       earth".^[901][181]
   32. [902]^ Invoked as the celebrant's mother: Syraja zemlja, - ty ž
       maty moja ("Moist Earth, you are my mother").^[903][185] The
       original text is thus: "Сира земля — то ж мати моя. / То ж то мати
       моя мене прийняла".^[904][186]
   33. [905]^ Professor Małgorzata Oleszkiewicz-Peralba stated that Polish
       and Russian languages refer to the earth and the rivers with this
       feminine epithet "little mother".^[906][188]
   34. [907]^ According to Russian scholarship, Bulgarian folkloric
       tradition uses the adjective "сура" (syra) "as a permanent epithet"
       of "земя" (earth).^[908][190]
   35. [909]^ The idiom is "drunken as mother Earth", speculated to be of
       [910]Proto-Slavic origin and presumed to relate to Earth as a
       passive element, that receives the frutifying rains of
       Heaven.^[911][197]
   36. [912]^ As an aside, Serbian philogist [913]Aleksandar Loma once
       suggested that the Slavic name for the [914]Verbascum, divizna
       ([915]cs), is a [916]dvandva compound of "*div" 'sky' and "*zma" (>
       zna) 'earth', harking back to the sacred mythological Sky-Earth
       pair.^[917][211]
   37. [918]^ Another possibility is that Khamyne was herself, in fact, a
       separate deity who was syncrethized with Demeter in later times.
       Her temple was discovered in 2006, 150 metres away from the main
       stadium.^[919][212]
   38. [920]^ Etymological connections of "[921]Thraco-Phrygian" Semele
       with Žemele and Žemyna have been noted.^[922][261] Thus, according
       to Borissoff, "she could be an important link bridging the ancient
       Thracian and Slavonic cults (...)".^[923][249]
   39. [924]^ Ivan Duridanov pointed out that the [925]Phrygian word
       zemelō also meant "Mother Earth".^[926][262]
   40. [927]^ The epithet also appears associated with minor deity
       [928]Iacchus, as in the expression Semeleios Iakchus plutodotas
       ("Son of Semele, Iakchus, wealth-giver").^[929][269]
   41. [930]^ Another Germanic reflex of "fold" is present in compound
       [931]Feldgeister ('spirits of the fields'), creatures of
       [932]Germanic folklore.
   42. [933]^ She is "the [934]Old Iranian goddess of cultivated land,
       vegetation and fertility, having a link with the rite of
       [935]inhumation" and to whom "the material earth
       belongs".^[936][300]
   43. [937]^ "In the realm of the material world, Spenta Armaiti is the
       guardian spirit of the earth (Vendidad 3.35), the symbol of
       bountifulness ... as well as the protector of herdsmen and farmers.
       Frequently, however, she is spoken of as the earth itself rather
       than as the genius of the earth (Yasna 16.10; Yasht 24.50; Vendidad
       2.10, 2.14, 2.18, 18.51, 18.64). ... in the physical realm she
       represents, and later becomes, the earth."^[938][301]
   44. [939]^ In Ahura Mazda's case, he is described as creator (or
       father) of Armaiti.^[940][311]
